| **F2-61** | found closing F2-60, 2026-07-31 | **F2-27 enforced the guard side of export authorisation and nobody ever reviewed the grant side.** F2-27's ratified premise is that *“the right to view one record on screen does not confer the right to extract the whole register as a file”*. It made 24 `MODULE:EXPORT` permissions actually enforce something — and those permissions were, and still are, granted to **32–55 roles each**. So the enforcement landed on a grant table nobody re-examined. 📊 ⚠ **CORRECTED 2026-07-31, twice over, by the second-model review — and both errors were mine, written the same day this row was.** **(i)** This said "dev and prod identical: SEVENTEEN" and listed `REGULATORY` among them. Re-measured: **dev 16, prod 17** — because **V333, shipped in the very same slice that filed this row, revoked `REGULATORY:EXPORT` from `VIEWER`.** The row was falsified by its own companion migration within the hour and still asserted it in the present tense. Dev's 16: AUDIT, BCM, CAPA, COMPLIANCE, CONTROL_LIB, CYBER, EVIDENCE, INCIDENT, NDI, PRIVACY, REPORTING, RISK, SCM, SUST, TPRM, WORKFLOW. **(ii)** This said the EXPORT permissions are "granted to 32–55 roles each", which **roughly triples the apparent breadth of the problem** and would have mis-sized the sweep. Measured distribution (dev, 26 EXPORT permissions): **only 6 are ≥32** — WORKFLOW 55, CHANGE 46, AI 42, SUST 33, NDI 32, COMPLIANCE 32 — and **14 are ≤14**: PLATFORM_ADMIN 0, PLATFORM 1, IAM 3, AUDIT_LOG 6, ADMIN 7, BOARD 8, AUDIT 8, INTAKE 8, PRIVACY 8, SCM 12, CONTROL_LIB 12, TPRM 13, REGULATORY 13, EVIDENCE 14. **The real shape is six wide modules plus the generic personas, not a uniform 24-module over-grant.** 🔑 **A row that cites a measurement is not thereby correct; it is correct as of an instant, and this one was overtaken by a migration in the same commit.** **D-10a, F2-59 and F2-60 are three symptoms of this one cause**, each fixed by narrowing one endpoint's guard. Fixing symptoms while the cause stands is the wrong shape — three instances is the point at which it stops being a coincidence. ⚠ **But this is a policy decision, not a defect fix, and it must not be swept.** “Which roles may extract a register as a file” is a product answer per module, and a blanket revoke would 403 paying users across 24 modules. The instrument that has worked three times is the **guard**, not the grant — and it worked precisely because each endpoint got a permission whose population already fit. ➡ **Proposed shape, for the owner:** (a) state the export-grant policy once — default deny for generic personas (`VIEWER`, `*_OWNER` asset roles), allow for the module's own operators and the assurance audience; (b) run `Appendix/scripts/grant_tightness.py` in the *grant* direction to produce the per-module delta; (c) revoke in module-sized slices with a before/after probe each, **not** one migration. ⚠ **D-10b's remaining revocation leg is an instance of this**, so it must follow the policy rather than precede it — see the re-ordered queue in §8 | M | 🔁 **(a) + (b) DONE 2026-08-01 — ADR-017 **D-10c**, spec now in the register, code-only, no migration, suite 2,748/0/0. (c) OPEN.** ⚠ **The amendment had to fix the rule before it could apply it: *“EXPORT follows READ”* is a comparison with no direction.** As a **ceiling** it narrows; as a **floor** it widens. Measured, the floor reading adds `CHANGE:EXPORT` and `REGULATORY:EXPORT` to `VIEWER` — and **`REGULATORY:EXPORT` is exactly what V333/V335 revoked from `VIEWER` three days earlier under this same rule.** So D-10c states it as a containment (`EXPORT` holders ⊆ `READ` holders) and adds that `EXPORT` never *implies* a grant. ✅ **And this row's proposed shape was wrong in the direction the second-model review predicted**: “default deny for generic personas” would have been a new principle contradicting the ratified one; the containment subsumes it without contradiction. 🚨 **The policy question had a live P1 instance inside it, found by measuring instead of arguing.** Three BCM export handlers authorised on `BCM:EXPORT` alone, and `CISO`/`CFO` hold that permission **without `BCM:READ`** — V320 Part 3 granted it to keep ONE AND-composed endpoint working, but **a permission is module-wide and a guard is not**, so it silently widened three guards nobody was reasoning about. Live before: `so_officer` → **403** on `GET /api/v1/bcm/plans`, **200 + 38,043 bytes** of that plan as PDF, **200 + 55,107 bytes** of the cycle evidence ZIP its own source calls *“the most sensitive export on the platform”*. **The file was the only way that user could see any of it** — F2-27's ratified premise running backwards. Live after: **403 on all three**, `auditor`/`compliance`/`rmanager`/`viewer` unaffected on screen *and* file, binder guard still admits `so_officer`. ➡ **Enforced in the guard, not the grant table**, because a grant edit is undone by the next grant edit while a composed guard cannot drift. ➕ **`Appendix/scripts/export_grant_policy.py`** is (b), re-runnable rather than a one-off report: per module it reports EXPORT==READ (9), EXPORT<READ (13) and the inversions (3), exits **non-zero on an *undocumented* asymmetry**, and carries the three exceptions with the clause that permits each. ⚠ **Two numbers in this row were falsified by it, both mine, both written the day the row was:** dev/prod `VIEWER` EXPORT is now **16/16** (the 16/17 split closed when V335 reached prod), and “32–55 roles each” is true of **6** of 26 while **14 sit at ≤14**. ➕ Filed in passing: **F2-78** — `/api/v1/bcm/binders/1/download`, the endpoint this whole exception chain protects, returns **500 for every caller including `admin`**. V320 granted two roles a module-wide permission to preserve access to something that works for nobody ✅ **(c) DONE 2026-08-01 — the remainder is closed, and it did NOT need slicing.** All **32** now AND a `READ` permission (33 counting `/board-pack.json`, whose identical guard `BoardPackGuardTest` asserts): **36 of the 38** `EXPORT`-guarded file handlers composed, **2** documented exceptions, `BARE_EXPORT_CEILING` **deleted** and replaced by `everyExportHandlerAlsoDemandsARead`. Suite **2,797/0/0**, FE 188/188, no migration. ⚠ **This row's own prescription — “revoke in module-sized slices with a before/after probe each, not one migration” — was right about grants and wrong about this.** Slicing exists because a revocation *removes* access and each loss must be argued; moving enforcement to the guard makes the collateral **provable** (`EXPORT ⊆ READ` in dev and prod → every principal that satisfied the old guard satisfies the new one), so there was nothing to revoke. 🔑 **The instrument you choose decides whether a change needs slicing at all — the deferral inherited its granularity from an instrument (a) had already replaced.** ➕ **The probe manufactured the defect rather than waiting for it**, which is what made it a probe at all: no live role was inverted for any of the 32, so a persona sweep could only have shown 200-before and 200-after. A `[REVIEW-TEST]` role holding **11 `*:EXPORT` grants and no `READ` anywhere** — V320 Part 3's accident, reproduced deliberately — took **33 of 33 exports with 200 and real bytes** (31,404-byte risk register, 40,404-byte board pack, 53,139-byte regulator ZIP, 68,686-byte IAM access-governance ZIP, 44,995 bytes of the tenant calendar) while refused **403 on all seven module screens that answer**. After: **0 of 33**, four real personas unchanged across **164 cells**, diffed by script not by eye. ➕ **Exception (i) got a real answer**: the board-pack exports compose **`ADMIN:READ`** — not a stand-in for the uncatalogued `BOARD:READ` but the guard `BoardPackController`'s own screen already carries, so the containment is stated against the actual read authority (8 ⊆ 21, both databases). ⚠ **Exception (ii) was over-broad and was hiding a handler**: the ceiling accepted *any* role list as narrowing, and `SecurityEventController#export` sat in that exemption with **no claim to it** — none of its four named roles holds `CYBER:EXPORT` without `CYBER:READ`, so it was excused from a rule it already satisfied for free. It now ANDs `CYBER:READ`, and the two genuine exceptions are **named** rather than pattern-matched. Third instance in this programme of a scan's exemption doing the work of a finding. **Mutation-proven twice** — a bare guard fails, and a bare guard *plus a role list* still fails. ➕ Filed and fixed in passing: **four F2-27-era guard comments asserted access-preservation in the direction that preserves nothing, and V335 falsified all four three days after they were written** — `NdiReportController`, `ClimateReportController`, `GreenFinanceController`, `BcmEvidencePackageController`. Corrected in place, because they are the notes a reviewer reads when deciding whether an export guard is safe, and all four would have argued this slice was unnecessary |
| **F2-62** | found auditing `iam.roles.is_active`, 2026-07-31 | **Deactivating a role removed no access, while the evidence pack told the auditor it had.** `is_active` was a **dead control**: written in five places (`createRole`, the revive branch, `updateRole`, `deleteRole`, `RoleRepository.reviveRole`'s native SQL) and read in two, and **neither reader was an enforcement point**. `CustomUserDetailsService.buildUserDetails` looped `user.getRoles()` and minted `ROLE_<name>` plus every permission without consulting the flag; only `user.is_active` gated anything. The second reader is `AccessEvidencePackageService`, which rendered `Boolean.TRUE.equals(getIsActive()) ? "ACTIVE" : "INACTIVE"` into the **Role Catalogue sheet of the pack handed to a SAMA/NCA inspector**. So the artefact leaving the building asserted a control that did not exist — and it was the half an auditor could actually check. `SodRuleRepository` filters `AND r.isActive = true`, so the platform knew the pattern; `Role` just never used it. ✅ **Zero collateral, measured before touching anything:** both databases have **0 live roles with `is_active=0` and 0 users holding one**, and `is_active` is `NOT NULL DEFAULT 1` with **zero NULLs**, so the fix reuses the pack's existing `Boolean.TRUE.equals` predicate and the two agree **by construction** rather than by coincidence. ⚠ **CORRECTED 2026-07-31 — and the correction matters more than the claim it replaces.** This row first read: *"all 58 live roles are GLOBAL, `mustOwnRole` rejects global roles as read-only, so `updateRole` cannot deactivate a single existing role — a control unreachable through its own UI is a control nobody can report as broken."* **That was measured on dev minutes after a concurrent session hand-applied V334 (F2-56, "system roles go global") to it, so it described a state that had existed for minutes, not the standing one.** Re-measured against **production, where V334 has not run: 10 global roles and 46 scoped to org 1** — and for org 1 `mustOwnRole` *accepts* those 46. So on production today a tenant admin **can deactivate 46 of 56 roles**, and the dead control was **reachable, not unreachable**. The defect was **worse** than this row originally claimed and the fix is worth correspondingly more; only the "why nobody noticed" story was wrong. ✅ **Unchanged by the correction:** 0 live roles with `is_active=0` and 0 holders in **both** databases, so the collateral really is zero, and the `NOT NULL DEFAULT 1` / zero-NULL finding that licences the shared predicate still holds. ⬅ **The transferable lesson is about measurement, not roles: a database reading can capture another agent's in-flight migration.** Three sibling worktrees were live (F2-56, F2-63, an orphan-discovery plan); V334 was applied by hand to dev and not to prod, so **the two databases disagreed about role scoping for the duration** — and I measured the scope split on dev alone. **Measure both databases before calling anything the standing state, and check the worktree list and `git log` for concurrent work before treating a dev reading as a baseline.** ➡ **Semantics decided: an inactive role confers nothing, and cannot be assigned.** Deactivation is **not** un-assignment (the `user_roles` row survives, so reactivation restores access) and **not** a way to disable an account (`user.is_active` still governs login). Enforcement is **one `continue`**, because `buildUserDetails` is the platform's only authority mint — the sole `new SimpleGrantedAuthority` site outside tests — and `ModulePermissionEvaluator` resolves both `hasPermission()` and the `ROLE_PLATFORM_ADMIN` bypass purely from the `Authentication` it produces. `JwtAuthenticationFilter` calls `loadUserById` **per request**, so deactivation lands on the **next request, same token, no re-login** — the same property SCR-002 documented for role revocation. ➕ Three provisioning paths refused loudly rather than storing an inert grant, which would have *relocated* the defect into the user editor: `UserService.assignRolesByNameOrId` (**both** the name branch and the legacy `roleIds` branch — an unguarded fallback is still an unguarded grant), `AccessRequestService.create`, and `BreakGlassService.activate`, the worst case — an operator told emergency access is ACTIVE, **mid-incident**, while holding no authority at all. ➕ Every report that *claims* to describe effective access was aligned, because leaving them would have reproduced the original defect one surface over: `effectiveAccess` no longer counts an inactive role's grants **or its admin bypass**, and gained `inactiveRoles` so an inert assignment is distinguishable from a live one instead of merely absent from the table; the pack's Status column now shares `Role#grantsAuthority` with the mint; the **Permission Matrix sheet gained a Role Status column** — sheet 1 calling a role INACTIVE was not enough while sheet 2 listed its grants in the same shape as a live role's, and the matrix is the sheet read as "who can do what". ⚠ **Two deliberate non-changes.** The SoD detective scan and the recertification snapshot filter `is_deleted` but not `is_active`: both describe **assignments**, which legitimately survive deactivation, and they now **over**-report rather than under-report — the safe direction. The `lod_band` JWT claim from `ThreeLinesService` is documented in code as a non-authoritative UX hint and gates nothing. Both are candidates, neither is this slice | S | ✅ **DONE 2026-07-31** on `fix/f2-62-role-isactive-enforcement` @`7f0051f7` — **no migration** (code-only; the column already exists and is already correct). Full suite **2,693/0/0**, FE typecheck clean, vitest **76/76**. **Proven live in both directions on the running stack** with a throwaway tenant role + user tagged `[REVIEW-TEST]`: **before** the fix, deactivating the role left `GET /api/v1/risk/appetite` at **200** — on the same token *and* after a fresh login — with `effective-access` still reporting the `RISK/READ` grant; **after**, `200 → 403` on the same token with no re-login, `403` after re-login, `inactiveRoles=[REVIEW_TEST_F262]` and `grants=[]`, assignment refused **400** *"Cannot assign inactive role(s) — they grant no access"*, and the real 68 KB pack ZIP carrying INACTIVE in **both** sheets. `RoleIsActiveEnforcementTest` pins the authorities half, that a **default-constructed role still grants** (the guard against silently revoking all 58 live roles that never set the flag), and the **biconditional** between the pack's ACTIVE/INACTIVE cell and the authorities actually minted — over real generated XLSX bytes, since each half looked defensible alone and that is exactly what let the defect live. **Verified the tests bite: 5 of 6 fail with the guard removed.** Cleanup by `CHARINDEX` delete restored the baseline exactly (58 live roles, 0 inactive, 0 holders); `platform.refresh_token` has **no FK to `iam.users`**, so an FK-walk cleanup would have orphaned 4 rows — found by scanning for the column instead of trusting the schema |
| **F2-63** | found browser-verifying F2-56, 2026-07-31 | **The permission-matrix editor cannot display two of the seven catalogued actions.** `AccessGovernancePage.tsx` declares `ACTIONS = ['READ','WRITE','APPROVE','DELETE','TRIAGE']` and renders one column each; the catalogue actually spans **seven** — those five plus **`EXPORT`** and **`BREAK_GLASS`**. Measured live on `CISO`: 40 granted permissions = READ 15, **EXPORT 13**, WRITE 7, APPROVE 4, **BREAK_GLASS 1**, so **14 of 40 (35%) are invisible** on the screen whose entire purpose is showing who can do what. Not data loss — `save()` sends `[...granted]`, seeded from the API response rather than from the rendered columns, so the hidden grants round-trip intact. The defect is that the console presents itself as the authoritative view while omitting an action class. ⚠ **And it omits precisely the one this programme has spent weeks narrowing** — F2-27, A17-D10a, F2-59, F2-60, F2-61 are all about `*:EXPORT`. An administrator opening this screen to ask *"which roles can extract our registers as a file?"* gets no answer, because the column does not exist. ⚠ Filed rather than fixed, because it is not a display change: adding an EXPORT column makes export grants UI-editable, which lands directly on **F2-61's open amendment**, and `BREAK_GLASS` is a privileged action with its own subsystem and may want read-only rendering. Read-only is strictly more capability than the status quo (invisible), and it is a one-constant flip (`ACTION_POLICY.EXPORT.editable`) when F2-61 lands. ✅ **Live-proven, and the anti-vacuity leg is the one that matters:** on `CISO` all 7 columns render (30 module rows, 40 grants, **the 14 formerly-invisible now shown**) — but as measured pre-rebase *every* cell there was disabled because CISO is shared, which would have made a locked EXPORT prove nothing. Re-probed on a **tenant-owned** `[REVIEW-TEST]` role: READ 27 / WRITE 28 / APPROVE 11 / DELETE 18 / TRIAGE 1 cells **enabled**, EXPORT 26 + BREAK_GLASS 1 **disabled**, Save enabled, shared-role notice absent — so the lock is the *action policy*, not the role. Then a real browser Save toggling `RISK/WRITE`: the DB shows `RISK_WRITE` **added** and `RISK_EXPORT` + `IAM_BREAK_GLASS` **preserved**. ➕ **A leg worth keeping: the whole-set PUT was proven to revoke what it omits** (a PUT carrying only `RISK/READ` left the role holding only that), so a "read-only column" would have silently meant "revoked on next save" had the payload not carried them — now pinned by test. 🧪 **`permissionMatrixActions.test.tsx` (9 tests; FE suite 85/85, tsc clean)** asserts the rendered column set **equals** the distinct actions in the response, generating its fixture from the **measured authority** — every `hasPermission('MODULE','ACTION')` literal in the backend (1,781 files, >900 guard sites) — so it cannot drift from what guards actually demand. **Mutation-proven both ways:** restoring the 5-element list fails 4 tests, forcing `isActionEditable → true` fails 3. ⚠ **The scan's first regex found SIX actions and would have certified the bug** — `hasPermission('INTAKE', 'TRIAGE')` carries a space, so a pattern without `\s*` reports that the platform has no TRIAGE. Third instance in this programme of *a scan matching too little excusing rather than finding a defect*; `\s*` is in the pattern and the population floors are asserted. ⚠ **This slice's own process keeper: master gained an F2-63 row while I was implementing F2-63.** I branched from `b790deb9`, filed my own row, and only found master's on rebase — two rows for one finding, which is the ledger-duplication this plan has already had to clean up once. Resolved by keeping **this** row and folding the disposition in, never by adding a second. The rebase also collided on the exact line F2-56 had just rewritten (`editable`), which is *why* the collision was useful: the two gates are now explicitly separate — `editable` answers "may this scope rewrite this role" (F2-56), `isActionEditable` answers "may this screen grant this class of authority" (F2-63), and a cell needs both. **Before implementing a filed row, `git log master` and re-read the row on master, not on your base.** ➕ **Two measurements in passing, neither in scope:** (i) **dev's role scoping is post-V334, not the standing state** — `TSS_GRC` holds 59 roles of which **58 are shared/global** because F2-56's V334 had been hand-applied to dev by a sibling worktree, while **prod (`TSS_GRC_Deployment`), where V334 has not run, still holds 47 of 57 at org 1**; so the matrix editor's reachability differs per database today, and F2-62's own correction note records the same trap. V336 got away with a verbatim copy because V172 still matched its entities; that was luck, not method. V338's columns were read off the `@Column` annotations, and it is schema-qualified and idempotent where the originals were neither. Live: `/api/v1/persistence/{key}` and `/api/v1/files` **500 → 200**, and a real write round-tripped (PUT inserted 2 rows, GET read them back, rows confirmed in the table, cleaned up to 0). ⚠ **DEV ONLY — status corrected 2026-07-31: this said ✅ DONE while all four tables are absent from every schema of production** (measured, not assumed). V338 applies on prod's next restart, deliberately batched with V337 rather than restarting twice for empty tables. **But "merged and dev-applied" is precisely what RC-B says is not done**, and writing ✅ here while declaring RC-B closed two sections above was the same mistake in the same document on the same day. The capability is live for nobody until the restart |
| **F2-72** | found fixing F2-71, 2026-07-31 | **Nothing measures column drift, and it is not theoretical — it produced a live 500 today.** A table can exist, be JPA-mapped, be classified `OPERATING` by the audit, and still fail on one column: `platform.persistence_row` was created with V12's column list and Hibernate rejected it immediately with `Invalid column name 'deleted_by'`. Neither `table_xref.py` nor `schema_drift.py` nor `SchemaContractRatchetTest` compares an entity's `@Column` set against `sys.columns` — all three stop at table granularity. ⚠ **The exposure is structural, not incidental:** dev applies V293+ **by hand via pyodbc with hand-split `GO` batches** while prod applies the same files through Flyway, so the two schemas can diverge column-by-column with nothing looking. Fix: extend the schema contract to columns — name, nullability and type per mapped field, reported as drift rather than asserted equal (a `@JdbcTypeCode(JSON)` on `NVARCHAR(MAX)` is legitimate, so equality is the wrong test). The app login's default schema is `dbo`, which is empty, so the query raised `Invalid object name 'flyway_schema_history'`, the `catch` logged it at **DEBUG** and returned `null`. ⚠ **Not cosmetic: `pending` is computed only in the branch where `appliedHead != null`, so in production `pending` was ALWAYS empty — and `deployment_identity.py` raises its "packaged but NOT applied" problem from exactly that field.** On the morning this was found, V337 and V338 were packaged-and-unapplied and that check reported nothing. ➕ **Why nine days of use did not surface it:** the script has **two independent legs**, and the working one masked the broken one — it still cried MISMATCH by comparing the artifact's head to the tree's, so the output looked alert while the database leg said nothing. 🔑 **The transferable defect is the sentence, not the schema:** the `null` branch was written for dev, where Flyway is off and the table genuinely is absent, and there its message is correct. In production the same `null` meant *"I could not read it"* and the text asserted *"nothing has been applied"* — **a read failure rendered as a statement about the world.** That is RC-A's two-authorities problem wearing RC-B's clothes: one code path serving two environments whose truths are opposite. ➕ And it is my own error twice over — my first pyodbc query hit `dbo.flyway_schema_history` and failed identically; I fixed my probe and never asked whether the application had the same bug | S | ✅ **DONE 2026-08-01** (code-only, **no migration**). Three changes, because one would have half-fixed it: the table is qualified with `spring.flyway.default-schema` (inert when unset, so dev is unchanged); `tableExists()` via `OBJECT_ID` separates **absent** from **unreadable**, and unreadable now reports `historyReadable: false` with the driver's own error instead of a reassuring sentence, plus a **WARN** when Flyway is enabled — a blind instrument must announce its blindness; and the error text is taken from the **root cause**, because Spring's own `getMessage()` is the useless half (*"select; bad SQL grammar"*) while the actionable sentence (*"Invalid object name"*) sits in the cause — "bad SQL grammar" would have told nobody the answer was a missing schema qualifier. Suite **2,717/0/0** (+4). **Mutation-proven by name:** reverting the qualification fails `theHistoryTableIsQualifiedWithFlywaysOwnDefaultSchema`; reverting either the absent/unreadable split or the root-cause extraction fails `anUnreadableHistoryIsReportedAsBlindness_notAsAnEmptyHistory`. Proven at the SQL layer against the **real prod database**: the query as it shipped raises `Invalid object name`, the qualified form returns **45 rows, head V336**. ⚠ **The endpoint itself still answers the old way until prod restarts** — which is F2-73 being an instance of RC-B while fixing RC-B's detector, and is stated here rather than glossed |

(2) **6** tables CREATEd twice under one name, the real CREATE always skipped behind `IF NOT EXISTS (sys.tables)`; (3) **F2-140** — 3 live tables and an unknown number of live columns that **no migration builds**, with F2-70 reading as closed because its ratchets only ask about *mapped* tables. **Fixed:** V2, V46, V47, V92, V102, V113, V120, V121, V150. **Chain now reaches V158 — 263 tables, up from V120/233 in one pass (38 files).** 🔑 **V121's demo fill was seven inserts that could never have inserted a row, behind a guard that could never fire.** Each sat in `BEGIN TRY — CATCH PRINT '— schema mismatch.'` under the comment *"Schema may differ; let the rest default. Guarded inserts with TRY."* **Both halves of that belief are false.** (1) `Invalid column name` is a **compile-time binding error for the whole batch** — SQL Server's deferred name resolution covers a missing *table*, never a missing *column* — so the batch never compiles and nothing inside TRY runs. **The CATCH has never printed.** (2) *"let the rest default"* is false: the omitted columns have no DEFAULT. Fixed with the value **every live row already carries**, not a new invention. ⚠ My first patch keyed on the audience literal and reached **3 of 4** tuples; the fourth is `RISK_COMMITTEE` and would have been a column-count error. *Counting the tuples afterwards is what caught it.* 🔑 **V150 declared four indexes and none of them exist anywhere.** `ix_security_event_sla_due` filtered on `status NOT IN ('RESOLVED','FALSE_POSITIVE')`, and a filtered-index predicate accepts **only simple comparisons joined by AND** — `NOT IN` is rejected at parse time. The statement never parsed, so **the whole batch failed and all four indexes were lost**: live `socops.security_event` carries its PK and unique constraint and **none of the four**, on a SIEM table queried by org+status, org+event_time, org+severity and SLA due. Rewritten as `<> — AND <> —`, semantically identical; a folder-wide scan confirms it was the only instance and that no filtered predicate still uses OR/LIKE/BETWEEN. 🔑 **V40 declared 8 committee types; the platform uses 22, and no migration ever extended the CHECK.** So a database built from the folder **rejected 14 types the application validates and actively uses** (17 are live in dev), and V159 could not seed `AI_ETHICS`/`CYBER_SECURITY`/`PRIVACY_DPO`. ⚠ **The two authorities anyone would compare were the two that agreed:** `CommitteeTypes.VALUES` and the live CHECK name the **same 22**, exactly. The drifting copy was the file nobody executed — RC-A with the third authority outside the comparison. Extended V40 from `CommitteeTypes.VOCABULARY` and gated it with `CommitteeTypeVocabularyContractTest` (2 checks, both directions + a floor so two collapsed scans cannot agree on empty). **That one CHECK unblocked 62 files: chain V158 → V220, 347 tables.** ⚠ Mutation-proven, and one claim in my own test was corrected: dropping `AI_ETHICS` fails it with the explanatory comment left in place, so the comment-stripping is defence for a future SQL-quoted comment rather than the thing that caught this. 🔑 **V221 had never run, and the reason is the same compile-time rule as V121.** It `ALTER TABLE ... ADD version_group_id` and then `UPDATE ... SET version_group_id = id` **in the same batch, with no `GO`.** Column names bind at compile time for the whole batch, so the UPDATE could not compile, the ALTERs never executed, and the file was a no-op reporting *Invalid column name*. **Three columns of attachment versioning have never existed in a migration-built database.** 🔑 **V228/V229 guarded the wrong half of their own precondition:** 67 index backfills asked `IF NOT EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM sys.indexes WHERE name = ... AND object_id = OBJECT_ID(t))` — when *t* is absent `OBJECT_ID(t)` is NULL, `object_id = NULL` is never true, `NOT EXISTS` is TRUE, and the batch proceeds to index a table that is not there. Hardened folder-wide (**67 guards across 16 files**, idempotent on re-run). ⚠ What surfaced there is **[[F2-140]] arriving from the other direction** — `compliance.compliance_assessments` is what blocked it, and this guard fix is deliberately *independent* of that row’s owner decision, because an index backfill failing on an absent optional table is a defect either way. 🔑 **V232 read a legacy register created 109 files later.** `compliance.policies` is created by **V341**, so a first ordered run had nothing to merge. Wrapped in `IF OBJECT_ID(...) IS NOT NULL`: a no-op is correct on a fresh installation, and reordering V341 would rewrite the schema history of every environment that has already run it. ➕ **The spine-ordering class is now enumerated and bounded: exactly 8 pre-V341 migrations reference a V341+ table** — V47, V120, V228, V229 and V232 are fixed; **V233, V302, V312** remain. **Chain V220 → V232, 349 tables.** ⚠ **Two of my own instruments failed this step, and both are worth the space.** An *ADD-then-use* static scan returned ~20 candidates of which nearly all were false: it cannot tell a column *identifier* from the same name inside a string literal in an `INFORMATION_SCHEMA` probe — and the decisive refutation was that the chain already passes every hit below V221. **The chain is the instrument; a static scan over SQL is not.** Then a generically-generated guard for V233/V302/V312 broke V233 syntactically, so it was **reverted rather than committed half-working** — those three need a per-batch guard, not a template. 🔑 **The spine-ordering class is closed: chain V232 → V320, 394 tables.** V233 and V302 each had exactly one batch reading a V341-created table; both now no-op when it is absent. **V312 needed nothing** — it names `compliance.frameworks` in prose only, which makes it the third comment-only false positive of the sweep. ⚠ **Two of my scripts disagreed about which batch to guard, and the cause is worth writing down because it wasted a revert.** One read the file with universal newlines and reported batch 3; the other read it with `newline=''` to preserve CRLF and reported **one** batch, so it wrapped the header comment and broke the file syntactically. The split pattern was `^[ \t]*GO[ \t]*$`, and a `GO\r` line does not match it — `chain_proof.py` works because it uses `\s*`, which includes `\r`. **Both scripts were right about their own input; only one was reading the file that is on disk.** Fixed to `[ \t\r]*`, and the reference-detection now strips comments before matching — the same discipline the register-orphan and localStorage checks needed. ➕ **Where the chain now stops is the boundary this row predicted.** V321 fails with `50321: REPORTING:WRITE is still held by no role` — one of **this programme's own verification THROWs**, firing correctly because upstream grant seed data is absent in a chain-built database. Not a defect: a guard working, exactly as the row's sizing note said (V321, V325—V329, V331, V332, V334, V335). 🔑 **Diagnosed rather than deferred: V321's THROW is not a seed-data judgement call, it is [[F2-143]].** A chain-built database holds **79 of 115 permissions and 54 of 58 roles**, missing `ORG_ADMIN` and the whole of `REPORTING:READ/WRITE/EXPORT`, so the grant matches nothing. **From here the remaining work changes character** — it is no longer *make the file runnable* but *decide what a fresh installation should contain*, because these guards assert on seed data. **V1—V320 of 360 now execute in order, from a folder where 279 files had never been run by anything.** ⚠ **My own column checker produced two false positives and they are worth recording:** it flagged `row_version` as an omitted required column on two inserts. `row_version` is a **ROWVERSION** — SQL Server assigns it and it *cannot* be inserted. **A checker that demands you supply an auto-generated column invents defects**, which is the fifth time this session one of my scans matched too much. ⚠ **BLOCKED 2026-08-06 (environment, not code): the C: volume has 0 bytes free**, and SQL Server Express keeps its data directory there, so `CREATE DATABASE TSS_GRC_ChainProof` fails with OS error 112 while expanding the log. The platform is not the cause — every TSS_GRC file totals under 1 GB against 490 GB used, and D: has 361 GB free. **Nothing on C: was deleted to work around it**; freeing that volume (or relocating the instance data directory to D:) is an operator action. Until then the chain proof, and therefore **F2-09**, cannot run. The unit suite and every document gate are unaffected. Instrument: `Appendix/scripts/chain_proof.py` |

| **F2-80** | found chasing a false lead during A17-D8, 2026-08-01 | **`GlobalExceptionHandler.handleAccessDenied` discards `ex.getMessage()`, so all **22** hand-thrown `AccessDeniedException` messages in the codebase are dead — including the four-eyes gates this platform sells.** Every one becomes *“You do not have permission to perform this action.”* The list is not cosmetic: `PolicyManagementService:413` throws **“Segregation of duty: the policy author/owner cannot approve their own policy”**; `ChangeManagementService:706` explains *“Recording the CAB decision requires CHANGE:APPROVE; CHANGE:WRITE authorises raising…”*; `CommitteeResolutionService:198` tells a non-member that a secretary or chair may record the vote on their behalf; `PlanDistributionService:152` names the designated recipient. **The user is told they lack a permission when the actual answer is ‘this is a maker-checker rule and here is who can’.** 🔑 **The codebase already holds the right principle and applies it to the sibling handler**: `handleBusinessRule` passes the message through, with the comment *“explaining the rule is the point”*. ⚠ **And the obvious fix is a new defect** — a blanket pass-through would leak `row.getRecipientName()` and `seat.getMemberName()` to an unauthorised caller, and Spring's *own* `@PreAuthorize` denials must stay generic so the guard expression is not echoed. So the split is real work: those 22 domain denials belong on the `BusinessRuleException` path (or a subclass), individually reviewed for what they disclose. ➕ The diagnostic cost is measurable too: it cost **this** slice a false lead — `admin` returning 403 on `crowdstrike/contain` read as a broken guard and was SEC-INT-003's tenant-inventory check, indistinguishable in both status and message | M | ✔ **DONE 2026-08-06** (no migration). All **23** domain denials moved to a new `DomainAccessDeniedException` whose handler passes the message; `handleAccessDenied` stays generic for Spring's own. **Live 4/4** — the finding's own repro now answers `"Falcon host is not in this tenant's inventory"`, and `ranalyst` on a BCM write still gets the generic string. Suite 3,326/0/0. ⚠ **23, not 22** — the spec predicted the 23rd and it had already been written. ⚠ Two messages named a person and would have started leaking the moment they became deliverable (`seat.getMemberName()`, `row.getRecipientName()`) — both now name the authority required; **and a test had pinned one of those names as required**, inverted. New `DomainAccessDeniedContractTest` (4 checks, all mutation-proven) includes an identity-interpolation rule, so the one-off review of 23 messages is now permanent |

There is none — so there is also nothing to provision roles, branding, a first administrator, licence rows, or an audit trail of who onboarded whom, and **a SaaS platform whose tenant count can only be changed by a DBA is not multi-tenant in the commercial sense**, whatever the code does with `organisation_id`. ⚠ The nearest thing to a workaround made it invisible: V97 seeded licences for `organisation_id = 1` as a **literal**, and each later tenant was hand-granted `SOCOPS` by its own migration, so three tenants exist and none of them arrived through a path anyone maintains | M | ✔ **DONE 2026-08-06 (V359)**. `POST /api/v1/iam/tenants` — one transaction: organisation + all **15** ratified SKUs + first administrator + audit row, rollback on any failure. **Live 15/15**, decisively: the new tenant's own admin logs in and sees **15 enabled modules**, so it is not fail-closed. Duplicate short code = 409 naming the field, writing nothing; `compliance` = 403. ⚠ **Two ratchets rejected the first design and both were right**: `platform`—>`iam` was a kernel—>business inversion (moved the feature under `iam`), and the `hasRole('PLATFORM_ADMIN')` guard pushed pure role sites 537—>538. ⚠ **The obvious ratchet fix was an escalation** — `PLATFORM:WRITE` is granted to `ORG_ADMIN`, a tenant admin; the guard is `hasPermission('PLATFORM_ADMIN','WRITE')` and V359 grants it so it does not rely on the code bypass. **Neither baseline was raised.** Two recommended steps were no-ops once measured (roles are global; branding self-defaults) |

| **F2-92** | found by the 2026-08-02 prod deploy's own smoke test | **The tenant write guard broke every insert in production, and nothing could log in.** `grc.security.tenant-write-guard.enabled` is hardcoded **true in prod and unset in dev**; with it on, every insert into a table carrying an identity/`ROWVERSION` fails with `A result set was generated for update.` `POST /api/auth/login` → **500**, because `platform.refresh_token` could not be written. **The last successful prod login was 2026-07-28** — five days, and it was our probe that found it. Pinned by **`TenantWriteGuardOnIT`** (4 tests, `it` profile — IT count 4 → 8): the real app boots with the guard ON, logs in, writes the refresh-token row, completes an identity insert, and still refuses a cross-tenant one. **The profile is eliminated too**: booted locally on the **`prod` profile** with the guard ON (SSL + Flyway off) — login **200**, `refresh_token` 2100 → 2101. Code, schema, DB options and profile all fail to reproduce it, so **the deployed runtime is the last variable** (Flyway-at-startup, the named-instance JDBC URL, the WinSW service account, the rest of `external.properties`). Narrowing needs the flag re-enabled in prod under supervision — an **owner decision**, and one now backed by four eliminated hypotheses rather than none. Until then prod has **no** Hibernate-level cross-tenant backstop. ✔ **CORRECTED + DECIDED 2026-08-06 — ADR-017 D-16, from artifacts nobody had opened.** `D:\Deployment\tss-grc\app\WinSW.NET4_20260803.out.log` shows production booting at 11:02:18 with `[TenantWriteGuard] interceptor WIRED` — a line `TenantWriteGuardConfig` emits **only** when `enabled == true`. **The `=false` written into `external.properties` was outranked and discarded**, because an imported document sits below `application.yml` and `application-prod.yml` outranks both. So the last sentence above is false: prod has had the backstop ON for **four days with zero occurrences**, 34 audit rows/day — the supervised soak this row was waiting for already ran, unobserved. ⚠ **P0 → P3:** the error appears **once**, on **one** login, in **24 days** of logs (`err.log` empty), and refresh-token rows were written at 11:32 and 11:34 that same morning **with the flag already true** — what changed at 14:23 was a deploy applying V337–V344 in-process, not the flag. ⚠ **"Verified working afterwards" is not in the data:** prod's newest `refresh_token` row is 08-02 11:34:17, *before* the 14:33 mitigation — **nobody has logged in since**, so the login path is unverified rather than proven either way, and it should be smoke-tested at the next restart. The stack trace, read for the first time, narrows the mechanism (`GetGeneratedKeysDelegate.performMutation` → `ResultSetReturnImpl.executeUpdate` → **driver-thrown**, SQL Error 0 / SQLState null) and both databases re-diffed **identical** on every result-set-producing dimension (1 trigger each on the same unrelated table, `refresh_token` 18 columns identical, no computed columns, no change tracking, no temporal table) — **still not root-caused, and no longer claimed to be.** The inert-override cause is **F2-149** |
| **F2-149** | found answering F2-92's owner decision, 2026-08-06 | **`external.properties` overrides placeholders, not properties — so the deployment's “only config file” cannot change most of the configuration, and says nothing when it fails.** It is imported by `spring.config.import` from `application.yml`; a **profile-specific** document outranks that and everything it imports, so any path `application-prod.yml` sets **literally** is silently discarded. 🔑 **18 of the 19 deployed keys work** because they are `SCREAMING_SNAKE` placeholder names the yml *consumes* (`${DB_URL}`) — a placeholder resolves at any precedence. **The one key written as a real property path is the one that failed, and it was the only line in the file in that style.** ⚠ Both config headers asserted the opposite in prose (*“raw spring keys work too”*, *“full override”*) and an operator acted on them during a P0. Measured blast radius: 23 literal booleans in `application-prod.yml`, **five of them `grc.*` runtime kill-switches** (tenant write guard + 4 SLA schedulers) that no operator could throw without a rebuild; and the template documented **38 of 59** knobs. Fix: the five become `${NAME:true}` (defaults unchanged, ON), headers corrected, template gains all 20 missing knobs, `DEPLOYMENT.md` §3 states the rule, and `ExternalConfigOverrideContractTest` gates all four properties | S | ✔ **DONE 2026-08-06** — suite **3,368/0/0**, **5 of 5 mutations**. ⚠ The 4th test was **broken when written** and only the mutation run caught it (it skipped its own check whenever the fix was present); rewritten as a positive assertion. ⚠ And my first count of undocumented knobs was **36, wrong by 15** — the regex read 20 commented examples as absent, which is *comment-as-absence*, the mirror of the trap this programme keeps catching. Deploying changes **no** behaviour: the stale raw key stays inert and the default is ON | — |

Two consequences: an examiner asking *"who authorised this emergency change?"* is answered from a log rather than the register, and **D-3's segregation-of-duties test had nothing to compare against** (F2-29 one lifecycle step later). Not back-filled from the ledger — that would manufacture provenance for a decision nobody can now attest to | ✅ **Fixed** — V345 adds `cab_decision_by_id`/`_name`, stamped from the authenticated principal on all three CAB-decision transitions, never from a body |
| **F2-94** | found by A17-D3's live probe — the escalation fired, was audited, and reached nobody | **126 of 164 automation rules notify nobody: the engine reads `to_role`, every seeded rule writes `roles`.** `AutomationActionExecutor.handleNotify` resolves the recipient from `action_json.to_role` (ESCALATE) / `role` (NOTIFY) — singular strings — while every rule seeded since V96 writes `"roles": [...]`, a plural array it never reads. Measured both DBs: **dev 126 of 164 codes / 153 rows; prod 125 of 161 / 150 rows**; only **12** rules deliver. Dead among them: `AR-97` HIGH-risk emergency change → CTO+CISO, `AR-72` expired policy → owner, `AR-73` → CRO+GC, `CHG-TASK-OVERDUE`, `POL-REVIEW-DUE`. **`audit_log` already holds 278 dev / 164 prod rows saying `has no role configured`** — the engine reported its own inertness for months. Root cause: the executor's javadoc claims the singular naming *"match[es] the conventional naming in the seed rules"*; it matches **12 of 164** | ✅ **Fixed 2026-08-03** (**V346**; suite **2,885/0/0**; live 19/20). The executor now publishes a **closed** vocabulary `RECIPIENT_KEYS = [roles, role, to_role]` read by one resolver, and a ratchet checks **every migration** against that same constant — so the fix is not one more key, it is the vocabulary being closed. ⚠ **The operational blocker I filed was measured false**: the feared "~150 rules' worth of notifications to real inboxes" is, in fact, **5 users in one tenant, one each** — **69 of the 84 role names these rules address do not exist in `iam.roles`**, and orgs 2 and 3 reach **nobody at all**. There was a second dead layer under the first, so turning delivery on was safe and the real deliverable was making the ghost layer *visible*: `GET /automation/notification-reachability` (new `AUTOMATION:READ`, which the catalogue also lacked — F2-53's shape, caught before shipping). **127 of 140 dead codes → 61**, every remaining one named with its reason. Produced [[F2-97]] |
| **F2-95** | found by re-measuring S6's premises | **The authorisation matrix demands roles on 33 of 36 rows and enforces none of them.** `change_authorisation_matrix.required_roles` is configured on **33 of 36 rows in dev and prod alike** and read in exactly one place — `out.put("requiredRoles", …)`, beside a `satisfied` flag computed purely on vote count. Its javadoc concedes it: *"advisory"*. ⚠ **The reason is that there is no vocabulary in which the check could be made**: `required_roles` names platform roles (`CISO`), rosters name governance seats (`Chair`, `Member`), and `approver_role` is free text the voter types (`Member — Cybersecurity`). No value from the first appears in the other two, so enforcing it literally would 409 **every** CAB approval — and `CTO` is a **ghost role** (F2-52's shape, 4th instance). The enforceable path needs no fourth vocabulary: F2-26's `member_user_id → iam.user_roles` (6 of 29 voting seats linked; `CISO` reachable, `CHANGE_MANAGER` not, `CTO` nonexistent) | ✅ **Fixed 2026-08-03 — both halves (S6b-i reported, S6b-ii enforced; V347).** Four states per role (SATISFIED / MISSING / NO_ACTIVE_HOLDER / NOT_A_ROLE) resolved through F2-26's `member_user_id → iam.user_roles`; `satisfied` is the conjunction. The 409 fires **only** on MISSING — and the split the row demanded was not a nicety: a literal gate measured **9 of 11** refusals in dev, **8 of 10** in prod. `CTO` stripped, not created. Live 14/14 |
| **F2-96** | same pass as F2-95 | **Both change-authority gates point at committees that can never be quorate — and health said fine.** Measured identically in dev and **prod**: org 1's CAB → committee 13 and ECAB → committee 12, both `ACTIVE`, both correctly mapped, **both with zero ACTIVE voting seats**, and `governance-health` answered **`healthy: true`**. A committee with no voting seat can never be quorate (`computeQuorum` sets `quorumMet=0` when `totalVoting==0`), so referred changes were undecidable and non-referred CAB-gated ones were recorded as *CAB approved* **in the name of a body with no members**. A17-D2's predicate asked "is a committee mapped?", not "can it decide?" | ✅ **Fixed** — the roster clause goes into `CONFIGURED_PREDICATE`, the **single** definition shared by the report and the enforcement path, so both changed in one edit (the F2-62 rule). New `ROSTER_EMPTY` fault, returned **before** `COMMITTEE_UNAVAILABLE`, with a remedy that names appointing members and says re-mapping is *not* the fix. ⚠ **Deliberate consequence: prod's demo tenant will have CAB/ECAB approvals refused after the next deploy until voting members are appointed to committees 12 and 13** |
| **F2-97** | surfaced by F2-94's ratchet — the one rule a closed vocabulary cannot excuse | **One automation rule addresses a person the engine can only address as a role.** `AR-149` (`COMMITTEE_ACTION_CREATED` → template `CRITICAL_ACTION_ASSIGNED`) names **no audience under any key** — the only NOTIFY/ESCALATE rule in the platform of which that is true. Its intended recipient is the *assignee of the committee action*, a per-user address that `NOTIFY` has no mode for, so a critical action assignment reaches its owner through no channel | ⬜ **Open — P3.** Filed rather than fixed because both quick closes are wrong: giving it a role fabricates intent (the template says *assigned*), and teaching NOTIFY to address a user is an F8 design decision. `NotificationService#notifyUser` already exists, so the work is small but it is engine work. Allow-listed **by name** in `AutomationRecipientKeyRatchetTest.AUDIENCE_ABSENT_BY_DESIGN`, which asserts the excused rule is still audience-less — the allowlist cannot outlive what it excuses |
> ⚠ **Status drift measured 2026-08-06, and the gate for it is deliberately NOT built.** Working F2-88 found it already implemented while its row read ⬜ **Open** and prescribed the exact fix present in the tree; F2-42 the same, with `buildBoardPack()` retired and a provenance gate over it while both its plan row and its register Status still said open. ⚠ **The first version of this note published a wrong figure, and the correction is the more useful result.** It said *"of 22 plan rows carrying an F2 id and an ⬜ marker, 17 are cited in code"*. That scan asked whether the row **contains** an open marker anywhere, and a row can be DONE while naming an open sub-item: F2-50 reads `✔ DONE 2026-07-29` and F2-53 reads `✔ CLOSED · ⬜ Still open: `visibility` is read by nothing`. It also assumed a fixed column, and these rows carry between **6 and 10** cells. Re-measured by finding the right-most cell that *begins* with a state marker: **15** rows are genuinely open, not 22, and **6 were over-counted** — F2-29, F2-41, F2-50, F2-51, F2-53 and F2-42. Of the 15, **10** are cited by name in source, tests or migrations; a citation is still not a closure (F2-30 has 23 and F2-77 is this programme's own live work). ⚠ **And 15 is a lower bound, stated as one:** **11** further rows (F2-05, F2-31—F2-37, F2-52, F2-55, F2-61, F2-68) have **no marker-led status cell at all**, so this instrument cannot classify them either way and they need reading by hand. 🔑 **Which makes this the fourth scan of mine in one session to match too much or too little** — after the F2-38 guard's two (blind to `'./userDirectory'`, and matching `localStorage` in its own comment) and the register-orphan check that counted a prose mention as a plan row. **The pattern is not carelessness about regexes; it is publishing a figure before reading the rows it came from.** A stricter comparison *was* built and **rejected on its own results**: comparing each finding's plan state against its register state flagged **6 of 38** comparable pairs, and reading them found **5 false positives** — because these findings do not have a binary state. F2-29 is *"AUTHORITY CLOSED · named-command split open"*; F2-41 is *"CLOSED for volume; classification tracked as an open decision"*; F2-49 is *"VISIBLE, disposition open"*. **A gate forcing one label onto a two-part truth reports the wrong answer 83% of the time**, and per `§1c`'s own rule a gate like that is switched off within a day — the same conclusion F2-01's prose count-gate reached with 69 false positives. **The negative result is the deliverable:** finding state is genuinely multi-valued, so the fix is per-row discipline, not a comparator. Only **F2-88** was an unambiguous plan-vs-register disagreement, and it is closed above; **F2-42** was a case of *both* documents lagging, which no comparison between them can detect — a defect two authorities share is invisible to any check that only asks whether they agree. **F2-50 and F2-51 were never stale at all**: the over-counting scan presented them as candidates and reading their status cells refuted it, which is the only reason no work was redone on them.

| **F2-143** | measured the moment F2-77's chain reached V320, 2026-08-06 | **A database built from the migration folder has an RBAC catalogue that is 36 permissions and 4 roles short, and one of the missing roles is the primary tenant administrator.** Measured by diffing a chain-built database against the live one: **79 of 115 permissions** present (**36 missing, 31%**) across **14 modules** — `ADMIN`, `BCM`, `COMPLIANCE`, `CYBER`, `PRIVACY`, `RISK`, `TPRM`, `SCM`, `BOARD`, `KB`, `FRAUD`, `INTEGRATIONS`, `AUTOMATION`, `REPORTING` — and **54 of 58 roles**, missing `ORG_ADMIN`, `VIEWER`, `RISK_ANALYST` and `FRAUD_INVESTIGATOR`. 🔑 **So a customer provisioned from the folder cannot be given the tenant-administrator role, and 36 permissions guard endpoints nobody but `PLATFORM_ADMIN` can reach** — and only via `ModulePermissionEvaluator`'s hardcoded code bypass, which is fail-closed and silent. This also undercuts **F2-81**'s tenant onboarding, closed earlier today: it provisions a first administrator, and `ORG_ADMIN` does not exist in a fresh build. ➕ **V321's THROW is correct and load-bearing, not an obstacle:** it fires because `REPORTING:WRITE` is not a catalogue row at all (a chain build has only `REPORTING:DELETE` — `READ`, `WRITE` and `EXPORT` are absent), so its grant JOIN matches nothing. **The guard caught exactly what it was written to catch, on a database nobody had ever built.** ⚠ **This was unmeasurable until today.** The catalogue is seeded by the Python bootstrap and by migrations that a baselined production never re-ran, so the two authorities anyone compared (the code's `@PreAuthorize` set and the live catalogue) agreed. Same shape as the V40 committee vocabulary: **the drifting copy was the folder nobody executed** | M | ⬜ **Open — derivation done, migration not yet written.** 🔑 **The permission half is now derived from sources alone, with no live database in the loop:** scanning `hasPermission('M','A')` across the compiled surface and differencing it against every `INSERT INTO iam.permissions` in the folder gives **32 pairs the code enforces and no migration catalogues** — whole modules absent (`BCM`, `CYBER`, `RISK` and `SCM` have **no** READ/WRITE at all; `COMPLIANCE` and `PRIVACY` have no READ/WRITE; `REPORTING` has none of READ/WRITE/EXPORT) plus `IAM:BREAK_GLASS` and seven EXPORT rights. ➕ **And zero catalogued-but-unenforced**, so the folder has none of F2-13b's anti-pattern — the gap is one-directional. ⚠ **The count is 32, not the 33 the scan first reported:** `hasPermission('MODULE','READ')` came from a **javadoc comment** in `AttachmentAccessPolicy`, not a guard. **Fourth time today a scan of mine matched a comment as if it were code**, so the rule is now explicit: strip comments before matching a name in source. *(That same javadoc calls it "the 94-permission catalogue"; it is 115.)* ⚠ **The role half's instrument failed and is not being used:** scanning grant statements for role names returned **67 candidates including `ACTIVE`, `DRAFT`, `PDF`, `HIGH` and `SUCCESS`** — status values and enum literals swept up by a three-uppercase-tuple pattern. Unusable, and reported as unusable rather than filtered by eye. The 4 role rows need a narrower derivation: the role names that appear in a **grant context specifically** (V321's `@grants` triple shape), which is internal to the folder and therefore not a copy from a live database of unknown provenance. ⚠ **The source-only derivation is REFUTED as a basis for generating the migration, and that is this iteration's result.** V361 was generated and then **deleted unshipped**, because the number it rests on is not reproducible: three implementations of the same scan returned **33, then 32, then 35** permissions. The moving parts were the comment-stripping and the set of actions treated as valid (`BREAK_GLASS` qualifies or does not, and that changes both sides of the difference). **A migration whose row count I cannot reproduce twice is a fabricated catalogue, however it was produced** — which is the exact class this row exists to close. ⚠ **The role half is worse and its own numbers say so:** the narrowed scan reports **29** roles the folder grants to and never creates, while the measured reality is **4**. So the role-*creation* extraction is missing shapes — V341's spine bootstrap and others insert roles in forms the regex does not match — and it overcounts by 25. It was not filtered by eye to look plausible. 🔑 **The instrument that IS reproducible is the one that produced the finding: diff a chain-built database against the live one.** 79-vs-115 and 54-vs-58 were stable and re-derivable. My stated objection to it — that the live database is a third authority of unknown provenance — was misapplied: provenance matters for deciding whether a row *should* exist, not for identifying which rows a fresh install *lacks*. **The correct design is a two-source intersection:** present live `AND` enforced by the code's guard set `AND` absent from a chain build. Each clause answers a different question, and the intersection cannot invent a row that no code enforces. 🔑 **The three-way intersection works and is reproducible: 34 permissions + 4 roles, two independent runs agreeing exactly**, with **2 deliberately excluded** — `PRIVACY:EXPORT` and `SCM:EXPORT` are live and chain-absent but enforced by **no code**, and cataloguing a permission that guards nothing is F2-13b's anti-pattern (15 such rows already exist). ⚠ **But generating it exposed two errors in this row, and neither is a detail.** **(i) The headline number is two defects wearing one figure.** Of the 36 pairs a V1→V320 build lacks, **13 are catalogued by genuine later migrations** (V327, V328, V329, V332, V333, V343, V346) — so they are not *uncatalogued*, they are **catalogued too late**, after V321 needs them. The remaining **~21 are catalogued by no migration at all** (`ADMIN`, `BCM`, `CYBER`, `RISK`, `SCM` READ/WRITE/DELETE, `COMPLIANCE`/`PRIVACY` READ/WRITE, and `REPORTING:READ/WRITE`) and are a permanent gap in any ordering. **Two defects, two different fixes.** ⚠ My measurement said 36 uncatalogued because the chain build stops at V320 and I read *"absent from the build"* as *"absent from the folder"*. **The instrument was right; the sentence I wrote about it was not.** **(ii) V361 is the wrong file, and a new version can never be the right one.** V321's THROW fires at 321; a fix at 361 runs 40 files too late, so a fresh installation still dies at V321. The fix must sit **below V321** — and it can, safely, because everything below the **V292** baseline carries no prod checksum. **The natural home already exists and is even already named for the job: `V272__rbac_catalogue_completion.sql`.** V361 was generated, found misplaced, and deleted — **the second time this row has produced a deleted migration, and both deletions were the right call.** ⚠ It also contaminated its own evidence: while present, it appeared in the scan as the cataloguer of all 34, which is why the *"catalogued by a later migration"* column had to be read with V361 excluded. ➕ **Next slice, now fully specified:** extend **V272** with the ~21 permanently-missing rows and the 4 role rows from the verified intersection; leave the 13 late-catalogued ones to their own migrations and instead ask whether V321's guard should tolerate running before them; apply V272 twice to dev via pyodbc; re-run the chain from V1 to confirm V321—V335 pass; then gate it. **Do not create V361** — and next free version therefore remains **V361**. ⚠ **Third attempt, third mechanical failure, and the pattern is now diagnostic: the derivation is settled and the EMISSION is what keeps breaking.** The intersection reproduces exactly (**34 permissions + 4 roles**, two independent runs agreeing) and V272 is the right file. But a script that appends to V272's `FROM (VALUES` list found the **wrong terminator** — V272 has several `) AS v` blocks (Part 1 catalogues permissions, Part 2 grants them), so the rows landed after a `;` and the file was syntactically broken. Reverted. The three failures were: an irreproducible count, a misplaced version number, and now an anchor that matched a later block — **none of them in the data, all three in the machinery for writing it out.** 🔑 **So the next attempt should not generate the SQL.** 34 rows appended to an existing `VALUES` list and 4 guarded role inserts is a **human-scale edit** — roughly 40 lines, into a file whose conventions are already established, with the row content taken from the verified intersection. Generating it has now cost three reverts and produced nothing; writing it once and checking it against a chain run costs less. **The instrument was worth building; the code generator was not.** ✔ **FIXED 2026-08-06 by hand, as specified: V272 extended with the 34 permissions and the 4 roles, and the chain moved V320 → V330.** V321, V324, V325, V327, V328 and V329’s guards all pass. 🔑 **Two ordering mistakes of mine, both caught by the platform’s own THROWs, and both worth recording.** (i) The role inserts named `is_privileged`, `is_system_role` and `description_en` — columns added to `iam.roles` by migrations LATER than V272, so the batch failed to compile. Reduced to the era-correct NOT NULL set; the rest take their defaults. (ii) I appended the roles at the END of V272, but Part 2 grants by `role_name`, so `ORG_ADMIN` received nothing and **V325 fired: “ORG_ADMIN lost WORKFLOW:DELETE — the derivation is wrong”** — one of this programme’s own guards catching an ordering mistake inside a fix for an ordering defect. Moved to Part 1 → roles → Part 2. ⚠ **And the chain then exposed a defect that had been latent in V272 since 2026-07-06:** it grants `WORKFLOW:APPROVE` to ORG_ADMIN, GRC_ADMINISTRATOR and RISK_MANAGER and `WORKFLOW:READ` to **none of them**, so each could decide a request it could not read. V324 has a guard for exactly that and it had never fired, because those roles did not exist in a folder-built database — the grants were silent no-ops. **Creating the roles is what made the guard able to speak.** READ added beside every APPROVE. ⚠ **Next: V331** — a board-pack audience of `EXEC` matches no `committee_type` (the vocabulary has `EXEC_COMMITTEE`); and the diagnosis is **not** a stale vocabulary. V331 already applies both normalisations (`CYBER_COMMITTEE` → `CYBER_SECURITY` and `EXEC` → `EXEC_COMMITTEE`), so the audience value is corrected before the check. The guard compares against `admin.committees.committee_type` **of committees that exist as rows**, and a chain-built database has no `EXEC_COMMITTEE` committee — V121 seeds a board pack for an audience the committee seed never creates. 🔑 **This is the seed-data class, and it is the first break that genuinely needs an owner decision rather than a fix:** either the committee seed should create an executive committee, or that demo pack should target one that exists. Both are choices about what a fresh installation contains, and inventing either would be the fabricated-seed pattern this programme keeps closing — **not** hand-typed and **not** copied from the live database, because the live database is a third authority of unknown provenance. `PermissionCatalogueUsageTest` already knows the code's set; a migration generated from it, plus role rows for the 4 missing roles, closes this and unblocks V321—V335's guards in one step. **This is the concrete content of [[F2-09]]** (schema convergence), which was blocked on F2-77 and is now unblocked | — |

For Sharia the ghosts are **the only surviving record of intended access** — delete them and the guard reads as a deliberate choice to give the module to compliance, which nobody made. ➕ Found only by reading `target/role-guard-sites.txt`, the reflection dump: a `grep` over `hasAnyRole(...)` returned **one** of these guards, because they span source lines — the trap `RoleGuardConversionRatchetTest`'s own javadoc records (F2-13a: 3 by grep, 73 by reflection) and which I walked into first | M | ✔ **ACCESS HALF CLOSED 2026-08-06 — V362.** ⬜ **Independence half deliberately still open, and the grant matrix is what proves it is.** **Decision: ADR-017 D-14 — three mechanisms, not this row's two options.** Measuring what the platform *already models* showed the eleven ghost names are not one kind of thing: **(1) seat the role** where the ghost names a job family nothing records — 9 of them, seated globally (`organisation_id` NULL, `is_system_role` 1, measured before choosing: seating is nine inserts, not a per-tenant exercise); **(2) bind to the record** where the platform already stores who-on-this-row; **(3) seat on a body** where the person holds a term-limited seat. 🔑 **`MODEL_OWNER` is a category error, not a missing role.** `ai_governance.ml_model.model_owner_user_id` exists and is **populated on 25 of 25 rows**, resolving to real `iam.users`. Seating a `MODEL_OWNER` role would have been **looser than the column** — the owner of model A could act on model B — so it becomes an entity-bound guard. 🔑 **The Sharia board is already a roster:** `sharia.sharia_scholar` holds 5 rows, `ssb_role` ∈ {CHAIRMAN, MEMBER×3, SECRETARY}, one TERM_EXPIRING, with terms, credentials, COI declarations and institutional emails — and **no `user_id`**, which is [[F2-26]]'s linkage gap. So `SHARIA_BOARD_CHAIR` stays a **declared gap rather than a fabricated role**. ➕ **`ai` does not belong in the seven**: it is the only one with a catalogued permission module, and its surviving population (RISK_MANAGER 23, CISO 20, DPO 19, AUDITOR 14) *is* a defensible AI-governance population — `COMPLIANCE_OFFICER` is on 5 of 26 sites, so the row's own criterion excludes it. **Six modules, not seven.** ⚠ Two other corrections: `ETHICS_OFFICER` is **22** sites not 23 (D-13 removed one), and `consumer` is the severe case — **PLATFORM_ADMIN and COMPLIANCE_OFFICER and nobody else on any of its 9 guards**. **V362** seats the 9 roles, catalogues **17** permissions (`SHARIA`/`ETHICS`/`OPENBANKING`/`AML`/`CPTY`/`CONSUMER`; **no `EXPORT`** — none has an export handler and a catalogued permission guarding no endpoint is F2-13b's anti-pattern) and writes **57** grants **derived from the guards, not authored**. ⚠ **The derivation caught itself being wrong**: classifying by METHOD NAME made `WhistleblowerCaseController#updates` — a `@GetMapping` whose name is the plural noun *updates* — a write, which would have granted `ETHICS:WRITE` to `INTERNAL_AUDITOR` on the strength of a regex. Reclassified by **HTTP verb**; `review` was then restored to the APPROVE shapes because `CoiController#reviewDeclaration` is a POST deciding somebody else's declaration, and its absence had left `ethics` with no APPROVE at all. The two matrices were **diffed**: exactly 3 cells changed, all three of them that endpoint's grantees. 🔑 **Seating is immediately effective with no code change**, because `hasAnyRole` compares names — **live 5/5**: a fresh user with no roles gets **403** on `/api/v1/sharia/scholars`, the same user holding `SHARIA_OFFICER` gets **200** (11 scholars) and **201** on the write guard, `SHARIA_AUDITOR` gets 200, and the seated role still gets **403** on `/api/v1/coi/campaigns` — licensed for this tenant but not granted — so the seating is scoped, not a blanket. `ShariaController` was **not touched**. ⚠ The probe's first leg-4 expectation was wrong (404 on a mistyped AML path, and 404 again on the real one because **only `ETHICS` of the six is licensed for org 1**); a 404 proves nothing about authorisation, so it was re-run against a module that *is* licensed. ⚠ **`ck_license_tier` allows only ENTERPRISE/PRO/FREE** — the `ck_ca_source` landmine again — and the probe's cleanup had to **delete** the licence row rather than disable it, because org 1 never had one. ⚠ **The ghost baseline falls 23 → 14, the largest single drop, and by the opposite mechanism to every previous one**: nothing left a guard — the roles arrived. The remaining 14 are exactly the categories D-14 excludes (seats, synonyms for real roles, record-bound, platform-wide identities), and the ratchet's javadoc now records that **a falling count is ambiguous**: a leg deleted and a role seated are indistinguishable from the number. Its pinned `ROLE_CATALOGUE` gained the 9 — **a stale copy would have invented 9 defects**, which is the direction worth stating. ⚠ **AND THIS ROW IS NOT CLOSED.** F2-145 makes two claims and this delivers one. The grant matrix, computed rather than asserted, shows **`COMPLIANCE_OFFICER`, `SHARIA_OFFICER` and `SHARIA_AUDITOR` all end with READ+WRITE+APPROVE** — three roles, one privilege set, which is not a separation of duties; same in `AML` and `CONSUMER`. Narrowing compliance out of Sharia approval, and splitting officer from auditor, **removes access somebody may be using today**, so ADR-010's Consequences require a per-tenant impact query first. It is a policy decision and it is left visible rather than smuggled into a migration. Suite **3,360/0/0** · ⬜ **Open — the independence half.** (a) A per-tenant impact query on `SHARIA:APPROVE`/`AML:APPROVE`/`CONSUMER:APPROVE` held by `COMPLIANCE_OFFICER`, then a decision on revoking it — the control F2-145 actually names. (b) Split `SHARIA_OFFICER` from `SHARIA_AUDITOR` so the auditor does not approve what it audits. (c) `MODEL_OWNER` → entity-bound guard on `ml_model.model_owner_user_id` (D-14 mechanism 2). (d) `SHARIA_BOARD_CHAIR` → waits on [[F2-26]]'s roster→user linkage (mechanism 3). (e) The `hasAnyRole` → `hasPermission` conversion for the six modules, which is [[F2-05]]'s backlog and now has an approved grantee list to convert **to** — and per ADR-010's new amendment must grant the **intended** population, never the surviving one | — |
| **F2-144** | found by F2-52's new existence gate, 2026-08-06 | **No employee can submit their own conflict-of-interest declaration, and the obvious fix would open an impersonation path.** `CoiController.SUBMIT_GUARD` is `hasAnyRole('PLATFORM_ADMIN','ETHICS_OFFICER','BOARD_SECRETARY','COMPLIANCE_OFFICER','USER')` and **two of those five names exist in no role** — `ETHICS_OFFICER` and `USER`, both in F2-52's measured 28. The class javadoc states the intent plainly: *"Declaration submission additionally allows `USER` (any authenticated employee can submit their own attestation)."* 🔑 **That capability has never worked.** Only `PLATFORM_ADMIN`, `BOARD_SECRETARY` and `COMPLIANCE_OFFICER` can submit — so a Nazaha/COI attestation campaign runs with the declarants locked out and three administrators able to file on everyone's behalf. ⚠ **And the fix F2-52's gate invites is worse than the defect.** Correcting the ghost names, or relaxing to `isAuthenticated()` as the javadoc implies, would be unsafe: `submitDeclaration` takes `userId`, `fullNameEn` and `fullNameAr` **from the request body** and stores them verbatim (`d.setUserId(req.userId())`) — it never binds the declaration to the authenticated principal. Opening the guard therefore lets any authenticated user **attest in another person's name**, which is the one thing a conflict-of-interest process must prevent. **The order is not optional:** bind the declarant to the caller first (ignore the body's identity fields, or 409 on a mismatch), *then* open the guard. ➕ Same shape as F2-53 (`KB_WRITE` naming a permission that is not a permission) and the same standing lesson as the `@NotBlank` episode: **before applying a gate's prescribed remedy, check what it does** | M | ✔ **PART (a) DONE 2026-08-06** · ⬜ part (b) open. The declarant is now chosen by the server: a holder of a COI management authority may still name any `userId` (filing on an employee’s behalf is the only behaviour that has ever worked here), everyone else declares for themselves and the body’s `userId` is **ignored rather than rejected** — a rejection would disclose whether another id exists. No principal at all is now refused: a COI attestation attributable to nobody is worse than a refused one. New `CoiDeclarantBindingTest` (4 checks), **mutation-proven** — trusting the body again fails 2 of 4 with the two ids named. ⚠ It also broke **5 existing tests**, and that is evidence rather than friction: `CoiServiceTest`’s auto-flag tests submitted declarations with **no authenticated principal at all** and passed, because the service stored whatever the body said. They now supply a declarant. ⚠ Name fields stay caller-supplied — bounded, not fixed: the row is attributed to a server-chosen `user_id`, so a mislabelled name is data quality against a known declarant rather than an attestation credited to someone who never made it. ✔ **PART (b) DONE 2026-08-06** (no migration — the point of the decision is that no permission is created). `SUBMIT_GUARD` is now `isAuthenticated()`, so an ordinary employee can file their own declaration: **live 9/9** — `viewer` (role `VIEWER`, zero ethics grants) POSTs **201**, and naming `userId=4` in the body still stores **6**, its own id, which is part (a) proven over real HTTP rather than in a mock; all four authority acts still **403** for the same caller, so the exception did not leak sideways. 🔑 **The exception is now a rule, not a favour.** ADR-010 gains **§Sanctioned exceptions** — *an endpoint discharging an obligation every employee is under is guarded by `isAuthenticated()`, not by a permission* — because **a grant list can only ever wrongly exclude someone**, and an employee who cannot file their declaration is a compliance failure that reads as the employee's fault. Three admission conditions, of which **server-bound subject is load-bearing**: without it the exception *is* an impersonation path, which is exactly why (a) had to precede (b). Two members, each with a companion test: KB reads (ADR-017 D-7) and this. Recorded as **ADR-017 D-13**. ➕ **The durable half is a different test entirely.** `ControllerAuthorizationRatchetTest` asserted `count <= 21` while its javadoc *enumerated* those 21 in three reasoned buckets — prose is not an assertion, and only the code runs. A ceiling tolerates the population it measures, so any member could have been swapped for a different unguarded endpoint and it would have read green: house rule 3's `BARE_EXPORT_CEILING` defect, found in the ratchet that house rule is about. Granting this exception was the **first request to add a floor member since the enumeration was written**, so the floor is now a **pinned set** asserted in both directions — it fails when a member gets guarded and is not removed, which a ceiling can never do, and **22 is derived** from the reviewed set rather than declared beside it. **Mutation-proven 4/4, each in its own test method** so no assertion masks another: drop the new member → the scan names it (so `isAuthenticated()` really is counted as unguarded, and the guard change really landed); add a member the scan can never produce → the stale direction fires; raise the anti-vacuity floor above the real **1,239** scanned write endpoints → it fires; change the pinned guard string → `CoiSubmitGuardTest` fires. ⚠ **`ETHICS_OFFICER` was deliberately NOT dropped** from `MANAGE_GUARD` — that would silently ratify compliance-officer ownership of ethics oversight, F2-145's reasoning applied to a second module. So removing two dead names from one expression moves the ghost baseline by **one** (24 → 23; `USER` had exactly one occurrence in all of `src/main/java`), which is the clearest illustration to hand that the ghost count is a *name* count, not a defect count. Read endpoints stay on the management role set — the javadoc already records row-level read scoping as a later slice | — |
